( Sal Pizarro/Bay Area News Group ) <p> A public memorial for Rod Diridon Sr. , considered the father of modern transit in Silicon Valley , will be held June 25 at the San Jose Civic Auditorium . <p> Diridon , a former Santa Clara County Supervisor and executive director of the Mineta Transportation Institute at San Jose State , died April 3 at age 87 . He is survived by his wife , Gloria Duffy , two children , Rod Jr . and Mary Margaret , and four grandchildren . <p> Duffy , CEO of the Commonwealth Club of California , said there had been initial hopes to have a celebration of Diridon 's life in May , but the later date was chosen to accommodate the schedules of many @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at 2 p.m. at the Civic , 135 W. San Carlos St. <p> Diridon was a native of Dunsmuir , Calif . His father , Claude Diridon , who worked in the railroad business in Dunsmuir when Rod was a boy , had changed his name from Claudius Diridoni because of bigotry in the industry . Still , the younger Diridon worked as a railroad brakeman and fireman to pay for Shasta Junior College and Chico State before he transferred to San Jose State , where he graduated with a bachelor 's degree in accounting . He served two combat tours in Vietnam as a U.S. Naval officer. , pausing his tour of duty only once to marry Mary Ann Fudge in 1964 . <p> Diridon was only 32 years old when he was elected to the Saratoga City Council in 1971 , and he toppled a 14-year incumbent three years later to become the youngest person elected to the Santa Clara County Board of Supervisors , where he served for the next 20 years . He campaigned for the county 's first half-cent sales tax for transit while he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to create VTA 's light-rail system and chaired the study that led to Caltrain commuter service . <p> He fought to save the historic train station on Cahill Avenue in San Jose after the 1989 Loma Prieta earthquake . The station was renamed in his honor when he left the Board of Supervisors in 1994 . He was the founding president of the nonprofit San Jose Trolley Corporation , now known as the California Trolley and Railroad Corporation . Its trolley barn at History Park in San Jose is also named after Diridon . <p> His first marriage ended in 1999 , and he married Duffy -- a former Deputy Assistant Undersecretary of Defense under President Bill Clinton -- in 2001 . Diridon became the founding executive director of the Mineta Transportation Institute , a think tank named for his longtime friend , U.S. Secretary of Transportation Norman Mineta , until he retired from that position in 2014 . Raising awareness about climate change was also one of his passions , and he regularly spoke to groups and led initiatives about the issue . <p> He served as president of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Herb Ritter -- district governor for Rotary District 5170 -- recently announced a new award to recognize climate change champions would be named in Diridon 's honor . In keeping with the environmental theme , the award itself will be made of wood instead of plastic . <p> Copyright 2026 The Mercury News . All rights reserved . @@137728191 <p> A growing number of Americans are trying to consume more protein . Dr. Ken Berry , a physician in rural Tennessee , traces some of the trend to people reporting improved health by following meat-based diets . <p> Each year , the International Food Information Council survey asks Americans to select the qualities that best define a healthy food . In 2025 , Americans ' views shifted , with " good source of protein " overtaking " fresh " as the top criterion . <p> The survey also found that for the fifth year in a row , most Americans ( 70% ) reported protein as the nutrient they 're most trying to consume . That 's up from 62% in 2021 . <p> While protein remains a priority for many Americans , nutrition experts continue to debate the optimal amount and sources of protein for long-term health . <p> Berry was the keynote speaker at Meatstock 2026 , an annual convention for followers of carnivore and ketogenic diets . <p> They all experience remarkable health benefits , according to Berry , who said he heard testimonial after testimonial @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reduced fatty liver and inflammation and generally transformed their health on the carnivore diet . <p> " The stories you hear at Meatstock grab your heart , and they also grab your brain and make you think , " he said . <p> Berry himself testified to dropping 70 pounds and reversing " a list of maladies , " including prediabetes , by making the switch from the diet prescribed by the American Diabetes Association to the carnivore diet nine years ago . <p> " I live on beef , butter , bacon and eggs , " Berry said . <p> The carnivore diet " sounds like basically a terrible idea , " Walter Willett , professor of epidemiology and nutrition at the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health , said in 2024 . <p> He said people miss out on fiber and other key nutrients and that eating animals could lead to climate change . <p> Others have criticized the diet for being too restrictive to be maintained long-term . <p> They have also pointed to a lack of clinical evidence they say is necessary to support the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's keynote speech has gained nearly 80,000 views on YouTube in three weeks . The theme of his talk was that people have been " misled and misfed . " <p> Some of the biggest misconceptions , he said , people believe about nutrition are that whole grains and fruit juices are good for you . They 'll keep you alive , he said , but they wo n't " optimize your health . " <p> Popular whole-grain foods such as bread and oatmeal are " almost uniformly inflammatory for most people , " Berry said , though many nutrition researchers have found associations between whole-grain consumption and improved health outcomes . <p> He added that the starch present in legumes , beans and other foods commonly accepted as healthy spikes people 's insulin . <p> Fruit juices found in many smoothies and other products labeled as wellness beverages " in many cases , have more fructose than Coca-Cola , " he said , though nutritional content varies significantly by product . <p> The World Health Organization 's ( WHO ) cancer research arm has classified processed meat as carcinogenic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continue to debate the strength and interpretation of the evidence . <p> Bacon , baloney and hot dogs can be health foods if the consumer is " an ingredient sleuth " and selects those made with only meat , salt and spices , Berry said . <p> Critics of carnivore diets have also raised concerns about the elimination of fruits , vegetables and other plant-based foods that provide fiber , vitamins and other nutrients commonly recommended in dietary guidelines .

May was not especially dramatic by weather standards , but it did leave behind two interesting signals : unusually mild nights and uneven rainfall . <h> What stood out in May <p> The month was not especially hot by afternoon standards . In many cases , the warmest May nights have clustered in recent decades , fitting a broader pattern of reduced overnight cooling that is strongly tied to climate change . <p> Rain was uneven , too . May included a couple of widespread light-rain events , then ended with a more focused late-month burst that dropped 0.60 of an inch in downtown San Francisco on May 28 , setting a daily rainfall record for the date . But the month was not broadly wet . @@137735392 <p> It 's 2030 , and the waters of Long Island Sound have crept farther up the shore , edging toward rows of towels and beach chairs . Fast forward to 2050 , and the rising tide is nearly knee-high on people strolling along the water 's edge . <p> By 2100 , much of the beach has vanished beneath the Sound . The headset user dips beneath the artificial surface , only to find themselves face-to-face with a saltwater fish swimming through an area that , a few minutes ago , was once dry sand . <p> An upcoming behavioral study by researchers at Fairfield University and Sacred Heart University will use augmented reality to determine whether seeing the effects of sea-level rise firsthand makes people more willing to contribute to projects that prepare them for it . <p> " Sea-level rise and climate change and all these types of events that are relatively new for us as a society are difficult to communicate to the average person , " said William Vasquez Mazariegos , a Fairfield University economics professor and one the study 's lead researchers . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at four coastal locations : Hammonasset Beach State Park in Madison , Sherwood Island State Park in Westport , Silver Sands State Park in Milford and Rocky Neck State Park in East Lyme . <p> Participants will be split into two groups . One group will wear a Meta Quest headset and view an augmented reality simulation developed by Fairfield University senior Mattia Speretta . The program overlays projected future sea levels onto the existing landscape , allowing visitors to watch the shoreline gradually transform as the water rises . <p> Afterward , participants will answer questions about their willingness to contribute money to climate adaptation projects and how much they 'd be willing to chip in . <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> Those projects could include living shorelines , beach nourishment and relocating park building away from areas vulnerable to flooding and erosion . <p> The second group will serve as a control group . Instead of viewing the simulation first , participants will read information about sea-level rise and its environmental impacts before completing the survey . They 'll get the chance to try @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We want to survey as many people as possible , " Mazariegos said . " We will be there in the morning and afternoon to capture as many people as we can from different backgrounds . " <p> Once they 've collected all the data , the researchers will compare responses between the two groups to see whether the 3D technology influenced people 's desire to donate . <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> The study builds on earlier research by members of the team looking at how climate information affects public attitudes . One key finding was that people often feel disconnected from broad , global discussions about climate change . When information is presented in a local context , however , respondents felt a stronger personal connection and become more supportive of adaptation efforts , Mazariegos said . <p> The Connecticut project will be the first in the series to use augmented reality . At its core , the study seeks to answer a simple but important question : Does making climate change feel real increase people 's willingness to take action ? <p> Previous @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ often based on local factors . <p> In research conducted in neighboring communities along Guatemala 's Pacific Coast , residents of the tourist community of Puerto San Jose were more willing to pay to protect beaches . Meanwhile , residents of nearby Iztapa showed stronger support for preserving biodiversity and coastal marshlands , according to Fairfield University physics professor Robert Nazarian , another lead researcher on the Connecticut study . <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> Those findings suggest that people are more willing to invest in protecting the places and resources they value most . <p> " What we 're teasing out is not just what people are willing to pay for and how much they 're willing to pay , but also the preferences which can vary in such close proximity , " Nazarian said . " That 's the part we 're working on now as we take on this next step of looking at coastal Connecticut . " <p> Jennifer Trudeau-Tham , an economics professor at Sacred Heart University , is the other lead researcher on the two-year project . It is one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Connecticut Sea Grant . <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> Researchers believe the findings could help shape environmental policy in the state . By identifying which projects resonate most with residents , they hope to provide local and state agencies with information that can guide future coastal management decisions . <p> The team is already looking ahead . Future versions of the project could allow users to visualize less obvious effects of climate change , such as shoreline erosion , while also showing how engineering solutions like seawalls could reshape coastal landscapes over time . <p> Austin Mirmina is a reporter with CT Insider where he covers the Long Island Sound , the environment , and sustainability among other topics . SpaceX now tells prospective investors in the IPO that data center buildouts are constrained by the " availability of power and water at economically feasible prices . " <p> The company goes on to say that " significant water resources may be required for cooling large-scale data center operations . " Water availability is such a concern that SpaceX says it has become a " critical consideration in data center site selection , development and operations . " <p> SpaceX also says that " water scarcity , drought conditions , competition for local water resources , or regulatory restrictions on water use could limit our ability to obtain sufficient water for cooling , constrain data center cooling capacity , increase our costs , delay or limit expansion of our data center infrastructure , or require us to implement alternative cooling techniques that may be more costly or less available . " <p> It 's not clear what inspired SpaceX to add this language about water to its filing , or why it was left out of the initial version . The company is in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Commission ( SEC ) has been sending SpaceX " comment letters " seeking clarification or additional details about the filing . It 's possible that questions from the SEC led to this particular change , though we wo n't know until those comment letters are made public in the weeks following the IPO . <p> Adding more detail about SpaceX 's access to water was not the only change the company made in this first amended filing . SpaceX also revealed that it is setting aside up to 5% of the stock being sold in the IPO for employees and friends of executives . @@137760081 <h> El Ni ? o is a naturally occurring weather phenomenon but its effects are worse because of climate change . <p> El Ni ? o will hit this summer with 80 per cent certainty , according to the latest forecast by the World Meteorological Organization ( WMO ) -- and Europe should brace for more extreme heat , with some areas at heightened risk of drought and flooding . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> Over the past week , parts of Western Europe suffered record-breaking spring temperatures as a powerful heat dome formed . Spells like this are likely to become more intense , longer and more frequent as El Ni ? o takes hold -- and scientists warn it could stretch all the way to 2028 . <p> Although the strength of the weather phenomenon is still uncertain , WMO models suggest it will be at least moderate , and possibly strong , with a 90 per cent chance of it continuing until at least November . <p> " The world must treat it as the urgent climate warning it is . El Ni ? o conditions will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " says UN Secretary-General Ant ? nio Guterres . <h> ' Prepare for hotter than normal temperatures ' <p> Fuelled by unusually warm ocean waters in the tropical Pacific , El Ni ? o is expected to leave virtually nowhere untouched , with above-average temperatures forecast around the globe for June to August . <p> " We need to prepare for a potentially strong El Ni ? o event -- which will exacerbate drought and heavy rainfall and increase the risk of heatwaves both on land and in the ocean , " says WMO Secretary-General Celeste Saulo . <p> The most recent El Ni ? o , in 2023-24 , was one of the five strongest on record and contributed to 2024 becoming the world 's hottest year on record . According to the European State of the Climate 2024 report , published jointly by the Copernicus Climate Change Service and the WMO , Europe experienced dramatic and contrasting conditions that year : while the east faced dry , scorching heat , the west endured heavy rainfall and flooding . <p> This year , " impacts will hit even harder @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ speed , " Guterres warns . <p> The UN has previously cautioned that there is an 86 per cent chance the coming years will smash 2024 's heat record , with climate scientists warning that a " whole range of extreme weather events " are brewing as a strong El Ni ? o collides with accelerating global warming . <p> While climate change is not thought to increase El Ni ? o 's frequency or intensity , it can amplify its effects . A warmer ocean and atmosphere increases the availability of energy and moisture for extreme weather events such as heatwaves and heavy rainfall . <h> How long could El Ni ? o last ? <p> Sea-surface temperatures began approaching El Ni ? o thresholds in late April to mid-May this year , according to WMO observations . Subsurface temperatures across the tropical Pacific are running more than 6 ? C above average , providing a substantial reservoir of heat feeding the surface warming . <p> The powerful naturally-occurring weather pattern typically forms every two to seven years and lasts around nine to 12 months . It usually reaches @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on global temperatures often strongest in the second year after development . <p> Its effects vary depending on intensity , duration , the time of year it develops , and how it interacts with other climate variables . <p> This year , above-average temperatures are forecast by WMO nearly everywhere in June , July and August . Below-average rainfall is expected across South Asia , the Greater Horn of Africa and Central America , where drier and warmer conditions are anticipated during critical growing and rainy seasons . <p> " Advance seasonal forecasts and early warnings are vital to save lives and cushion the impact on our economies and our communities , " says Saulo . The time for informed decision-making , planning and preparedness is now , the WMO adds . <p> Guterres urges action on the human-caused elements of climate extremes , calling for " ending the addiction to fossil fuels and accelerating the shift to renewables " . 
@@137760189 <p> Thank you for visiting nature.com . You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS . To obtain the best experience , we recommend you use a more up to date browser ( or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer ) . In the meantime , to ensure continued support , we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript . <p> Video-first social-media platforms such as Instagram , TikTok and YouTube are replacing those that are based mainly on written content.Credit : hapabapa/Getty <p> The world is undergoing a generational shift in the way in which news is being reported and disseminated . Social-media platforms have upended who can make news content and at what cost . Information exchange is becoming faster , more visual and more personal . Short bursts of content compete for people 's attention in algorithmically curated feeds , increasingly generated by artificial-intelligence tools . The boundaries between news , opinion and entertainment content are becoming blurred -- as is the dividing line between fact and fiction . <p> This could be one of the most marked shifts in how people @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in printed media in the twentieth century . Researchers and scientific publishers need to better understand what is happening and engage and respond appropriately . The future of fact-based communication -- including science communication -- is at stake . <p> The speed and scale of the shift is being monitored by the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism at the University of Oxford , UK , among others . For the past 14 years , the institute has produced a respected Digital News Report that tracks global trends in news consumption . In March , it examined more than a decade 's worth of findings to better understand one demographic : people aged between 18 and 24 years ( see go.nature.com/4dhn96t ) . Individuals in this group are now more likely to get news through social media , rather than through news websites and apps . Video platforms -- mainly Instagram , TikTok and YouTube -- have displaced the likes of Facebook as the media platform of choice . Individual news creators are favoured over established news brands -- some of which are perceived by this demographic to be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " , according to the report . <p> In this increasingly competitive environment , it is essential that credible science broadcasts a strong signal . There are many content creators doing excellent work . But , as Nature 's news team reported in a News feature in February , many influencers with large followings are promoting misinformation -- for example on climate change , vaccines and health and wellness ( see Nature650 , 542 -- 544 ; 2026 ) . <p> Last year , public-health researcher Brooke Nickel and her colleagues reported an overwhelming amount of misleading information about medical screening tests from an analysis of nearly 1,000 Instagram and TikTok posts . They also found that the people who produced these posts often had financial interests in the tests ( B. Nickel et al . JAMA Netw . Open . 8 , e2461940 ; 2025 ) . In February , humanities scholars Ricardo Morais and Clara Fernandes found that videos produced by science influencers on TikTok tend not to credit sources -- including for images -- making it difficult to assess the accuracy of their posts ( R. Morais @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A03 ; 2026 ) . <p> These are among the reasons why more researchers and science communicators , those who have the knowledge and skills to convey science in line with research integrity principles , need to be on these platforms ( see Nature635 , 8 ; 2024 ) . As our News feature shows , many scientists are . Nature has a well-established presence on Instagram and YouTube ; a few months ago , we also joined TikTok . <p> Short videos have their strengths and limitations . An average three-minute video could contain up to 650 words of script and captions . In a sense , that is not a lot of time to convey the content of a finding or news event , including sources , significance , context , caveats and limitations . But done well , narrative storytelling , infographics , animation and video are all incredibly compelling and popular ways to engage people with science . <p> But improving science communication should n't be the responsibility of only those in front of the camera . Platforms need to be doing much more to curb @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ practice in science communication , including how to navigate possible conflicts of interest , and flagging claims that have not been verified to users . <p> Morais and Fernandes also recommend that trusted science organizations should work with social-media companies to improve platforms ' policies . The World Health Organization and TikTok partnered up in such a way during the COVID-19 pandemic to reduce misinformation about the SARS-CoV-2 virus . <p> Barring unforeseen developments in media technologies -- which can not be discounted -- social-first and visual content is likely to become the dominant form of news . If science communicators want to remain relevant , they need to embrace this change .

It is calculated based on a Morningstar Risk-Adjusted Return measure that accounts for variation in a managed product 's monthly excess performance , placing more emphasis on downward variations and rewarding consistent performance . The top 10% of products in each product category receive five stars , the next 22.5% receive four stars , the next 35% receive three stars , the next 22.5% receive two stars , and the bottom 10% receive one star . @@137792683 <p> Mayor of London Sadiq Khan speaks to members of the media at Prior Weston primary school before delivering a speech on his plans to tackle climate change to environmental groups and members of the media at Barbican Centre in 2021 in London . <p> Mayor of London Sadiq Khan speaks to members of the media at Prior Weston primary school before delivering a speech on his plans to tackle climate change to environmental groups and members of the media at Barbican Centre in 2021 in London.Leon Neal -- Getty Images <p> Around the world , particularly in the U.S. and Europe , countries are in the middle of a climate reset . Right-wing politicians want to nix climate policy full stop . Their left and center-left counterparts are backtracking on promises for aggressive new policies under the assumption that climate is no longer popular -- or perhaps that it never was . <p> Sadiq Khan , the London mayor who completed a decade in office last month , says his tenure offers a different lesson . His climate policy push has generated moments of significant blow back from right-leaning @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ observers to cast green policies as all-but-inevitable instigators of climate backlash . And yet Khan survived and his policies did too . " There is a silent majority who are n't keyboard warriors , " he told me in April . <p> Every country , province , and city has a different texture , but it 's a reminder that ditching climate entirely may not be necessary or wise in this moment of reassessment . " These policies , green policies , environmental policies , can be popular , " Khan says . <h> Future Proof <p> There was no greater backlash than in 2023 when Khan pushed through an expansion of the city 's Ultra Low Emissions Zone ( ULEZ ) , a program that charges Londoners with vehicles that do not meet certain emissions standards for driving across the city . What was meant as a technocratic method of reducing air pollution and addressing climate change turned into a culture war focal point . Protesters tore down and disabled enforcement cameras and painted the policy as a civil liberties issue . Other opponents dismissed the air pollution science @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disinformation , misinformation ... gave a vocal minority massive airtime and gave people the impression this was an unpopular policy , " he says . <p> Advertisement <p> Calmer heads complained that the policy would increase the cost for commuters and small businesses that bring vehicles into the heart of London . The issue received wall-to-wall coverage . Watching from afar especially , it felt as though the effort had become a central political issue in Britain . <p> But the reality was more complicated . A poll conducted the year after implementation , around the time of the 2024 mayoral election showed that voters ranked the issue ninth on a list of priorities . <p> In Khan 's telling , a lot was on the line when he was up for reelection in 2024 . Leaders in other cities that were in various stages of considering a similar policy -- including New York and Milan -- were watching closely . Defeat might have signaled a political cost not just to pollution charges specifically but to climate policy more broadly . He won with an even greater share of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Khan , who serves as the co-chair of C40 cities , a group of cities committed to climate action , explains his climate policy success in part by focusing much of his public message on kitchen table issues rather than climate specifically . He explained ULEZ as a health matter rather than a climate measure . ( Indeed , air pollution in many cities including London is a silent killer ) . " People do n't talk about climate change , climate emergency , environment ... What they do know is the young child 's been diagnosed with asthma , " he says . " <p> And he has sought to connect clean energy with affordability and cost savings at a time when constituents in London and voters around the world are concerned with the rising cost of living , particularly energy . In many cases , targeted strategies -- such as blocking viral entry or modifying a smaller set of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ whole genome recoding . <p> A more ambitious and potentially transformative goal would be to define the minimal human genome -- stripping it down to the smallest set of genetic elements required for a cell to function . The focus would switch from large-scale editing to gaining a deeper understanding of which elements are essential . <p> The human genome is roughly three billion bases long , but only about 2% of it encodes genes . The rest is a sprawling , partly characterized mix of sequences required for gene regulation , genome structure and other as-yet-unknown functions . Much of the genome is plausibly dispensable , although we do not yet know how much . <p> The potential benefits of a synthetic minimal human genome are substantial . <h> Enjoying our latest content ? Mancini said King 's Beach has been known as " the dirtiest beach in the commonwealth " due to poor stormwater drainage . But in 2025 , it was swimmable on nine out of 10 days . <p> A number of factors might have contributed to the improved water quality at King 's Beach , said Mancini , including a drier than average summer , ongoing infrastructure improvements and an initiative using ultraviolet light to disinfect the water . <p> He said more data is needed to determine which had the greatest impact , but " it was probably a combination " of all of them . <p> In a statement , state Sen. " <p> The state Department of Public Health posts results online from tests of water quality at both ocean and freshwater beaches from mid-June through Labor Day . <p> Despite infrastructure improvements , rain and runoff are still among the biggest contributors to ongoing beach pollution . These sewer overflows can worsen water quality along Boston 's rivers , beaches and waterways . <p> Mancini recommends waiting at least 24 hours after a heavy downpour to spend a day at the beach . @@137792799 <h> Scientists are injecting elm trees with a killer fungus to save them <p> Walking down into the floodplain at the Nature Conservancy 's preserve in Benson , Vermont is like walking into a sea of American elm saplings . <p> The trees criss-cross about 30 acres on the valley floor in tight rows . <p> " American elm is a foundation tree species in floodplain forests , " says Leila Wilson , an ecologist with the U.S. Forest Service . " These are systems which are facing severe threats from non-native pests and pathogens , but we also know from other impacts , right ? Land use change , conversion to agriculture , now climate change , and changing precipitation and temperature patterns . So these are systems that are in peril . " <p> Wilson knows these trees well . Each one comes from a seed she harvested from a tree whose flowers she isolated with little plastic bags , then hand-fertilized using pollen collected in the lab . <p> That pollen came from big old elms scattered across the Northeast . <p> Mature American elm trees like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hundreds of years old . ( Abagael Giles/Vermont Public ) <p> After all that care , Wilson is in Benson to pump these baby trees full of a fungus that 's likely to kill about a third of them . <p> " We 're going to be infecting them with the Dutch elm disease fungus . So drilling a little hole into each tree , and then injecting a solution that contains 100,000 spores of the fungus into each tree , " she says as she drills the first hole . <p> It 's bittersweet , she says . <p> " I feel like we should warn them and apologize to them , " she says wistfully as she works . " Because we 're torturing them . And it 's not nice . I do n't like that part . " <p> Big , old elms are rare these days . But that was n't always the case . <p> Leila Wilson , an ecologist with the U.S. Forest Service , holds up a syringe full of spores of the fungus that causes Dutch elm disease , next to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( Abagael Giles/Vermont Public ) <p> If you took a walk along the banks of a river in Vermont a hundred years ago , you 'd likely find yourself looking up mostly at towering American elm trees . About 50 years ago , Dutch elm disease wiped out most of these trees , but a few survived . Now , scientists like Wilson want to use these survivor trees to bring American elms back . <p> The disease is caused by a fungus that 's spread by beetles that feed on elm twigs . From there , the fungus infiltrates the tree 's vascular system , clogging up vessels called xylem that carry water up into the leaves , a little like arteries . <p> As the fungus spreads , the tree cauterizes the vessels in an effort to stop it . This makes the disease snowball , until the leaves and the tree 's canopy die . <p> It 's a little reminiscent of heart disease in humans . <p> Scientists are n't entirely sure why , but some trees appear to be more resistant than others . It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ could also be that the beetles have a preference for certain kinds of trees . <p> Regardless , scientists hope that by identifying and then breeding these resistant individuals , they can bring more big American elms back to the landscape of the East Coast . <p> American elms in a hedgerow along a neighboring field . ( Abagael Giles/Vermont Public ) <p> Wilson is hopeful that many , if not most , of the saplings at this site in Benson will survive this test . She and her colleagues will look for survivor trees by rating the lushness of their canopies . <p> All in all , it will take about two years to know for certain which trees made it and which ones succumbed . <p> The ones that survive will have their seeds harvested to be used to grow a nursery stock that can be planted as part of floodplain restoration projects around the region . <p> American elms are of particular interest to scientists because they are big , long-lived trees with roots that can survive being underwater for weeks at a time . Mature elms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ along flood-prone rivers , in ecosystems scientists say are increasingly important to protect as human-caused climate change consolidates rain into more extreme deluges across the region . <p> When elms died , green ash moved in to replace the role they filled . But those trees are increasingly under threat from emerald ash borer , and are dying en masse . <p> A 2022 University of Vermont study found that planting trees like elms in floodplains could save Vermont $1 billion over the next century and cut flooding damage by almost 20% . <p> When elms are killed by Dutch elm disease , they lose their canopy like this one in Benson . ( Abagael Giles/Vermont Public ) <p> Gus Goodwin is a scientist with the Nature Conservancy who is caring for the elms in Benson . From the nursery , he points out an adult elm that was killed by Dutch elm disease , on the border of the neighboring hayfield . <p> The top of the tree is curled up and bare , like a witch 's broom . <p> Goodwin says losing an iconic tree like the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ecological one . <p> " I think because it 's such a beloved piece of our cultural history and planted throughout so many towns and so many of our civic spaces , there 's that kind of like community loss too , " he said . <p> Just over the fence from the nursery , he points out a big , healthy American elm . This one could be hundreds of years old . Shaped like an overflowing vase , it towers over the saplings the scientists are inoculating with spores , and serves as a reminder of what used to be . <p> " There 's just something special about walking into a big forest with big trees , that 's messy , that has all this kind of complexity and diversity , " Goodwin said . @@137793689 <p> SEATTLE -- A portion of one of the most ambitious ocean monitoring networks ever built will go dark this month when scientists board a research vessel and motor off the Oregon coast to pull a research buoy from deep out of the Pacific . <p> The buoy 60 feet below the water 's surface will be removed June 16 from the Ocean Observatories Initiative -- a network of more than 900 ocean sensors built at a cost of $386 million that has continuously collected real-time data for more than a decade . But last month , the National Science Foundation announced it would dismantle most of the system , pulling instruments from waters off Oregon , Washington , Alaska , North Carolina and Greenland by 2027 . <p> Funded by the foundation , the observatories have tracked everything from ocean circulation and marine ecosystems to climate change and extreme weather . Its data has been freely available and has informed more than 500 scientific publications . The project was slated to run for another 15 to 20 years . <p> In an emailed statement , the foundation said the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " aligned with a " wider strategy of a nimbler approach to prioritize support for evolving scientific priorities and emerging technologies , as well as smart lifecycle management within its research infrastructure portfolio . " The foundation added that its decision drew in part on a 2025 National Academies report on the future of ocean science . <p> But for the scientists who built and operated the system -- and the researchers , educators and students who rely on its data -- the timing feels particularly punishing . <p> An El Nino event , which disrupts weather patterns and supercharges marine heat waves , is predicted to arrive along the Pacific coast this summer . One marine heat wave is already pushing unusually warm water off California . <p> Without the Oregon and Washington moorings and the network of underwater gliders the Ocean Observatories Initiative operated in the region , researchers say they 'll lose much of their ability to measure what 's happening below the surface , which is precisely where the most significant oceanographic signals are . <p> " It 's a crippling loss of information , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ helped lead the initiative 's Pacific Northwest operations , told The Associated Press Tuesday . Scientists can get some data from the surface , such as temperature and the distribution of chlorophyll , which drives photosynthesis in plants , but information below can not be gathered from satellites alone , including low oxygen zones . <p> The initiative launched in 2015 after more than a decade of community planning and construction . It was designed as a 25 to 30-year project , built in part around the oceanographic consensus that detecting meaningful climate signals requires at least three decades of continuous data . " We 've just got to the 10 year record , " Dever said , " which will give you some hints , but it wo n't continue on . " <p> One significant piece will remain : a seafloor cable network managed by the University of Washington off the Pacific Northwest coast , which will continue providing data on volcanic and seismic activity in the region . <p> Scientists had seen warning signs as the administration 's proposed 2026 budget included a 55% cut to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in early May . <p> The initiative was coordinated by the Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution in collaboration with the University of Washington and Oregon State University , as well as past partners including Rutgers University and Scripps Institution of Oceanography . <p> The initiative operated on roughly $48 million a year , not including the cost of research vessels , which adds substantially to the overall price . Prior to budget cuts , which began in 2025 , around 60 to 70 people worked directly on the project across its partner institutions , Dever said . <p> " What 's happening with the Ocean Observatories Initiative is not unique , " he said . " This is just one of a number of science facilities that is being dismantled at the present time . It seems to really mark the end of a federal commitment to basic scientific research -- a commitment that has served this nation very well for the last 70 years . "

@@137810285 <h> Data Center Operators Are Trying to Fix Their Water Use Problems <p> Google , Microsoft , and other hyperscalers have come under scrutiny for their impact on water quality and availability . <p> Photograph : Jason Doiy/Getty Images <p> On Monday , SpaceX amended its initial public offering to state that water conditions -- including water scarcity , regulations around water , and drought -- could constrain data center development . <p> It is n't the only tech company trying to assess how water scarcity might impact its business . Water use is emerging as one of the most contentious data center issues . A recent Gallup poll found that seven out of 10 Americans are opposed to data center development , with water scarcity ranking as the top resource concern . Facing increasingly fierce resistance , some tech companies are scrambling to assure the public that they 're facing the issue head-on . <p> Data centers primarily use water to cool server racks , which throw off massive amounts of heat . One popular technique , known as evaporative cooling , uses fresh water to absorb the heat @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ evaporates outside . <p> Using more water can save money and reduce emissions for big tech companies by reducing the power needed for cooling that relies on energy-intensive pumps to recirculate water . But it can also come with a large water footprint : Google 's facility in Council Bluffs , Iowa , for instance , which uses evaporative cooling , consumed more than 1 billion gallons in 2024 . <p> Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory predicted in a 2024 report that hyperscale data centers could consume up to 33 billion gallons of water by 2030 if they relied heavily on evaporative cooling . That 's on par or even less than other thirsty industries , like agriculture or oil and gas -- a single fracked well can use 1.5 to 16 million gallons of water -- but it poses a risk in regions where water is already scarce . The risk is particularly acute in summer , when data center cooling needs tend to skyrocket at the same time as municipal water use . <p> " Water is a highly local , highly regional issue , " says Shaolei Ren @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It 's a limited resource , and we have to manage it very carefully . " <p> Some tech giants , including Microsoft , OpenAI , and Oracle , have made statements in recent months indicating that they are moving away from evaporative cooling entirely in order to save water . That includes OpenAI and Oracle 's massive Stargate expansion in a number of states , including a water-stressed region of Texas . <p> Google is taking a different approach . On Wednesday , the company rolled out a series of water-related commitments to communities where it has data centers , along with funding announcements for water-related projects in the US . <p> They include pledges to replenish more freshwater than the company consumes , via investments in local water projects ; to scale up the use of reclaimed and recycled water ; and to disclose annual water use in data centers . ( Other tech companies , including Microsoft , have similar promises around water replenishment and local investment . Google has been working on most of these pledges for a few years . ) There 's also a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what data center designs would work best with local watersheds . <p> Ben Townsend , the global head of infrastructure and sustainability at Google , says that data center design is a lot more complicated than simply swearing off one type of cooling in all cases . The company , he says , has been doing detailed hydrologic assessments of its sites for the past four years to determine what types of cooling would work best . <p> " Water is scarce in some regions and plentiful in others , " he says . " A one-size-fits-all strategy just does n't work . " <p> In April , Google defended evaporative cooling for areas with what it called " abundant " water in a filing to the European Union as necessary for developing truly sustainable data centers . Google 's arguments line up with new research from Ren and his team , who found that if all data centers in the US were to adopt some kind of evaporative cooling during peak demand , it could free up an additional 10 to 30 gigawatts of power . In areas where @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ evaporative cooling could provide a meaningful headroom to utilities trying to balance load . <p> " If you do n't use water , the challenge is that you 're going to be using a lot more power in the summer , and that will push up the cost , " Ren says . <p> Most tech giants , including Google , have seen their carbon emissions skyrocket as a result of the AI boom . Totally avoiding evaporative cooling could increase emissions if data centers rely on dirty energy to keep facilities cool . Using less evaporative cooling could also mean more water used offsite for electric generation , depending on how data centers are getting their electricity . <p> Despite efforts to curb water use , tech companies are still struggling to do so -- and it could eventually impact business . Even as Microsoft is moving away from evaporative cooling , The New York Times reported in February that the company 's internal records indicate that its water use is set to skyrocket . In 2024 , Google halted plans for a data center outside of Santiago , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ water concerns . ( The permits for that data center were granted in 2020 ; Townsend says the company adopted its water scarcity framework for new locations a few years after that . ) <p> In 2021 , Google funded a lawsuit filed by a town in Oregon fighting a local newspaper to avoid disclosing how much water the tech giant would use for an expansion of its existing data center . The company began disclosing water use from specific data centers in annual reports in 2023 . <p> Priscilla Johnson , an independent consultant who served as Microsoft 's director of water strategy between 2017 and 2020 , agrees with Ren that there 's trade-offs between water and power . However , she says , there are ways to push companies to develop better designs that use both less water and energy . Public pushback and regulation , she says , is crucial . <p> " The industry has to be challenged to design smarter and simplify things , " she says . <p> Molly Taft is a senior writer for WIRED , covering climate change , energy , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and editor at Drilled , an investigative climate multimedia reporting project . Echoes of this argument will resonate in everyone 's lives . One side focuses on efficiency : building more conveyance , freeing up markets and moving water to its highest-value use . The opposite side insists that any reform must start from justice : the human right to safe water , tribal rights and the support to those communities already living with contamination and scarcity . <p> A major new working paper from the National Bureau of Economic Research , " Measuring Water Misallocation in California , " speaks directly to this debate . Via detailed empirical work , it shows that California 's water system is ill-suited to the climate realities ahead . <p> Using extensive satellite imagery , crop evapotranspiration data and field-level planting decisions , the authors mapped the productivity of water across the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ consistently more valuable south of the Sacramento-San Joaquin Delta than north of it . The authors also show that , because so much water is locked into long-lived orchards and vineyards , very little can be reallocated in any given year without major economic losses . <p> Advertisement <p> These findings describe not just inefficiency but also the rigidity of a system that should adapt more flexibly under climate stress . <p> For climate justice advocates , these findings quantify what many frontline communities have already experienced : that California 's water rules are not flexible or responsive enough to share risk fairly in drought . Rather , they tend to protect those with senior water rights claims and long-standing investments , while exposing everyone else to greater risk of shortages or contamination . <p> The paper also illustrates the limits of an efficiency-only lens , which would focus mostly on irrigators @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people most visible in that analysis would be those who already have water , capital and legal recognition . If we stop at so-called efficiency , we will use that map to move more water to the same powerful actors who have always benefited . If we start from justice , we can use the same evidence to start an entirely different conversation about what California 's water system is for and whom it must serve in a warming world . <p> David @@137826191 <h> When El Ni ? o becomes an economic crisis <h> El Ni ? o is not merely a weather disturbance but a development challenge that can transmit climate shocks through heat stress , agricultural uncertainty and food inflation , exposing the fragility of India 's informal economy ; as climate risk increasingly becomes economic risk , stronger adaptation through heat-resilient cities , worker protection and better water management is essential <p> India often meets the arrival of extreme heat and erratic rain with a familiar shrug : the weather is harsh , the monsoon is uncertain , and life must go on . But that response misses the larger point . If El Ni ? o returns as forecast , India will not face merely a weather disturbance ; it will face a development crisis in which heat stress , water scarcity , crop losses , and food inflation expose the fragility of the informal economy . <p> The U.S. National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration 's ( NOAA ) ENSO Diagnostic Discussion Report(2026) states that El Ni ? o is likely to emerge soon , with an 82% chance @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the winter of 2026-27 . India 's own weather office , the India Meteorological Department ( IMD ) , in its Long Range Forecast for the Southwest Monsoon Season(2026) , has projected monsoon rainfall at 92% of the long-period average , placing it in the " below normal " category . <p> That matters because , in India , climate shocks do not remain confined to the atmosphere . They move quickly into the labour market , the mandi , the household kitchen , and the city street . A weak monsoon is not simply a concern for meteorologists ; it is a warning that rural incomes may weaken , food prices may rise , and working hours may shrink . For a country where a large share of employment remains informal and climate-exposed , El Ni ? o is best understood as an economic transmission mechanism . <h> The heat economy <p> The firstchannel is heat . Long and punishing summers reduce the productivity of workers who can not escape outdoor exposure -- construction labourers , delivery riders , street vendors , and agricultural workers . Climate change affects @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ economic security . A hotter India is not merely a warmer India ; it is an India where earning a livelihood becomes increasingly difficult . Heat stress lowers productivity , reduces working hours , and deepens income insecurity for millions who depend on daily wages . <p> The secondchannel is agriculture . A 2026 Reuters report on India 's monsoon outlook noted that the southwest monsoon supplies nearly 70% of the rainfall needed to water crops and recharge reservoirs and aquifers . When rainfall becomes uncertain , sowing decisions become riskier , irrigation costs increase , and groundwater extraction intensifies . For small and marginal farmers already struggling with volatile prices and rising input costs , climatic uncertainty magnifies economic instability . In that sense , El Ni ? o is not merely a climatic event ; it is a shock to the production base of the rural economy . <h> The price shock <p> The third channel is inflation . Food prices are often where climate stress becomes visible to every household , not just farming communities . According to the Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation 's Consumer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in April 2026 , raising concerns that a weaker monsoon could intensify price pressures further . If rainfall weakens and crop stress deepens , price pressures can quickly spread across vegetables , pulses , and other essentials . For policymakers , this creates a difficult balancing act : the same climate shock can simultaneously weaken growth and intensify inflationary pressures . <p> El Ni ? o is not merely a weather event but a development challenge . India needs stronger climate adaptation measures through heat-resilient cities , worker protection and better water management . Climate risk is now economic risk , and its burden falls most heavily on the poor . <h> Comments <p> Comments have to be in English , and in full sentences . They can not be abusive or personal . @@137829698 <h> Proposed cuts to satellite funding are a threat to science GUEST COMMENTARY <h> Share this : <p> This image provided by NASA shows an Earth-mapping satellite , the first collaborative mission between NASA and the Indian Space Research Organization , launching from India on Wednesday , July 30 , 2025 . ( NASA via AP ) <p> This World Environment Day , we should look up to the skies and say thank you to the nearly 1,500 hardworking Earth observation satellites that help us understand our changing planet . <p> There are things about Earth that we can only see and study from the vantage of space . Through world-renowned agencies like NASA , NOAA and the National Center for Atmospheric Research , the U.S. heavily invests in remote sensing technologies , cutting-edge missions and world-class scientists , including at the Johns Hopkins University Applied Physics Laboratory in Laurel . As a result , data from space is interwoven into all aspects of our lives , from the air we breathe to the water we drink and the food we eat . <p> Over half of the information we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cover and water vapor levels -- comes from space . This vantage point gives us insight into the amount of tree cover and whether a wildfire is starting more efficiently than we could relying solely on ground instruments . <p> Earth observations also play a key role in " science diplomacy " -- in other words , by supporting partnerships between nations centered on scientific exchanges that improve lives . The United States is typically the nation of choice for these partnerships because of our advanced space capabilities . <p> Just to give you an example , Surface Water and Ocean Topography , a collaboration between NASA and the French space agency , measures almost all of the water on Earth every 21 days . This helps states and localities decide when to declare a drought , sparking conservation measures , as well as aiding farmers in using water more efficiently . <p> But these public investments are under attack . The Trump administration has recommended aggressive cuts to satellites that monitor the environment multiple times -- including as recently as April . <p> The rationale provided is that these @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ agree with that statement or not , it ignores the real-world benefits these systems deliver for everyday Americans , from farmers to fishers to families in areas impacted by natural disasters . <p> It also ignores the rising costs of climate change . Leading economists estimate that the average family is paying $400-900 annually due to the changing climate . <p> The Trump administration has claimed that the private sector can take over important observations , including weather forecasts . To be clear , U.S. commercial space systems and nonprofit-managed satellite constellations are important and integrated parts of the space ecosystem . They help power the space industry , which is the envy of the world and is poised to contribute trillions to the economy over the next five years . <p> Together , commercial and publicly funded systems form a mutually beneficial ecosystem for Earth observations . Bear in mind that government purchases are about 75% of the market today for remote sensing , and industry relies on data from NASA satellites to calibrate , validate and build trust in their products . <p> It goes without saying that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be behind a paywall . Imagine having to buy another subscription , this time to protect your life and property . <p> Last year , Congress rejected the administration 's proposed cuts and ensured the environmental data Americans rely on continues to be available . This took a heroic advocacy effort that climate and space organizations are gearing up to fight again this fiscal year . <p> At the end of the day , we only have one planet , and we need data from space to help us understand it . That was true during the Apollo 8 mission when the iconic Earthrise photo was shared with the world , and it remains true today for the Artemis generation . The mission patch worn by the four astronauts who traveled to the moon bears the Earthrise image . And the zero-gravity meter for the Artemis II mission -- RISE -- is the Moon with a cap of the Earth , which was inspired by the Earthrise photo and designed by a second grader in Mountain View , California . <p> We ca n't let President Donald Trump take away @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ eyes in the sky . <p> Mary Guenther is head of space policy at the Progressive Policy Institute . Susie Perez Quinn served as chief of staff at NASA . <p> Copyright 2026 Baltimore Sun . All rights reserved . " <p> The leadership change arrives after founder and former president Jess Devaney moved to Perspective Films , a production shingle launched by the Perspective Fund . <p> " Anya has been a key architect of Multitude 's strategy , programs , and body of work , " Devaney said in a statement . " She brings a rare combination of political clarity , creative instinct , and disciplined leadership , and a deep commitment to the filmmakers and movements we engage . @@137885782 <p> KIAWAH ISLAND -- In an unusual and unprecedented turn of events , the town of Kiawah and the S.C . Department of Natural Resources have removed two coyotes from the private island community after they killed three nesting loggerhead sea turtles . <p> The now-culled coyote 's behavior was highly atypical . Michelle Pate , sea turtle coordinator with DNR , said in a statement there is no recorded history of coyote predation of mother turtles in the United States , calling the behavior an " unusual escalation . " <p> " By all accounts , this is extraordinarily unprecedented behavior , never before recorded , " Kiawah Island Mayor Bradley Belt said at a June 2 town council meeting . <p> The Post and Courier 's Rising Waters Lab focuses on impacts of climate change and related policies and practices . It is supported by donations and grants to the nonprofit Public Service and Investigative Fund , whose contributors are subject to the same coverage we apply to everyone else . For more information and to make a donation , go to **25;523;TOOLONG sign up for our newsletter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which adult sea turtles are at their most vulnerable , the only occasion in their life that forces them to emerge from the sea . The loss of a nest is manageable ; sea turtles have evolved over millions of years to lay multiple nests , often with more than 100 eggs in them , as a way to hedge the species ' bets against Mother Nature . <p> Coyotes are known to eat sea turtle eggs . That loss , while unfortunate , is part of the evolutionary roulette . The loss of a mother sea turtle has a much larger impact on conservation and recovery efforts for the threatened species , Pate said . It takes about 35 years for a female sea turtle to reach sexual maturity and begin laying eggs . <p> Before this incident on Kiawah , the scientific literature contains no record of coyotes attacking mother turtles , said Jim Jordan , wildlife and natural resources director for the town . In 2025 , a dead turtle appeared on the beach a couple of times , and it 's possible that coyotes were involved @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ At the June 1 town council meeting , officials initially thought the turtles were attacked by sharks , Belt said . <p> Pate later said there were three attacks in 2025 , which officials confirmed were caused by coyotes . Then in May 2026 , two turtles were killed in quick succession on Kiawah 's beach . <p> Coyotes began colonizing Kiawah Island in 2009 . In 2026 , a male and female began attacking mother sea turtles as they prepared to nest on the island 's beaches . <p> Jordan was overseas on vacation , providing input via email , as the town and DNR figured out a plan . DNR asked the town to remove the offending coyotes , and the mayor approved the operation on the Friday before Memorial Day , Belt said . <p> Before the team could respond to the issue , another loggerhead turtle was discovered on the beach after a coyote attack . It was transported to the South Carolina Aquarium for treatment , but did not survive , Belt said . <p> Asked about the the turtle sent to the S.C . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ referring The Post and Courier to DNR . <p> On May 24 , DNR personnel spent the night on the beach , and killed two coyotes . Since then , no coyote attacks on adult loggerhead sea turtles have been reported , Belt said . <p> DNR reported that an alpha male and female were the coyotes killing the turtles , and the juvenile coyotes on the island had not managed to learn this new behavior yet , he said . <p> " Public safety and the protection of threatened wildlife remain top priorities as the agency continues monitoring and response efforts , " Pate said in a statement . <h> Coyotes on Kiawah <p> It is not unusual for coyotes to dig up nests and eat sea turtle eggs . Of more than 22,000 sea turtle eggs laid on Kiawah Island beaches in 2025 , about 10 percent were eaten by coyotes . The number has increased in recent years , as the coyote population on the island has rebounded . <p> The number of eggs eaten by coyotes pales in comparison to the situation in the 1960s and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ turtle eggs laid on the island , Jordan said . <p> To prevent coyotes from reaching the eggs , members of the sea turtle patrol , an organization that documents and manages sea turtle nests on the island , place a metal screen over the nest , Jordan said . Coyotes ca n't get in , but baby turtles can get out . <p> " That is pretty much 100 percent effective at , you know stopping that depredation after the first night , " he said . <p> Coyotes first arrived on Kiawah Island in 2009 . While the species has not historically made its home on the island , individuals slowly moved into the region after red wolves were eliminated from the state in the early 20th century . <p> Starting in 2012 , the coyotes became more noticeable on the island as individuals began competing for territory . Once a few established themselves on Kiawah , the numbers leveled off , Jordan said . Then , in 2015 , coyote numbers dropped significantly on the island , which Jordan suspects was the result of rodenticide poisoning . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from putting out the poison , and coyote numbers have since rebounded . Today , there is once again a stable population of the species on the island , Jordan said . <p> The town 's coyote management plan largely involves letting the population coexist with people and other animals on the island . It is nearly impossible to eliminate coyotes from an area , as the species produces more pups when their population diminishes . <p> Until May 2026 , there was never a reason to do " lethal control , " Jordan said . <p> Editors note : This story has been updated to correct the year of previous coyote attacks on sea turtles to 2025 and add confirmation of those attacks . 
@@137913988 <h> The Brief <p> The Ocean Observatories Initiative , a network of over 900 ocean sensors that has informed more than 500 scientific publications , is being dismantled . <p> The National Science Foundation announced it is " descoping " the OOI and will remove instruments from waters off Oregon , Washington , Alaska , North Carolina and Greenland by 2027 . <p> It 's unclear who decided to dismantle the program , but scientists had seen warning signs as the Trump administration 's proposed 2026 budget included a 55% cut to the science foundation . <p> The Ocean Observatories Initiative , a sophisticated network of more than 900 ocean sensors that track everything from ocean circulation to climate change to extreme weather , is being dismantled by the Trump administration . <p> The project , which is funded by the independent federal agency the National Science Foundation , was supposed to run for another 15 to 20 years . Now , scientists and researchers who rely on the data are concerned about the timing as they prepare for a strong El Ni ? o event along the Pacific coast @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we know : <p> The National Science Foundation announced in May that it is " descoping " the OOI and will remove instruments from waters off Oregon , Washington , Alaska , North Carolina and Greenland by 2027 . <p> It 's unclear who made the decision to dismantle the program , but scientists had seen warning signs as the Trump administration 's proposed 2026 budget included a 55% cut to the science foundation . <p> What they 're saying : <p> Michael England , a spokesman for the National Science Foundation , told The New York Times the decision " aligns with N.S.F. 's wider strategy to have a nimbler approach to prioritizing support for evolving scientific priorities and emerging technologies as well as a deliberate approach to smart life cycle management within its portfolio of research infrastructure . " <p> " It 's a crippling loss of information , " Ed Dever , a professor at Oregon State University who helped lead the initiative 's Pacific Northwest operations , told The Associated Press . <p> Scientists say they can get some data from the surface , such as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in plants , but information below can not be gathered from satellites alone , including low oxygen zones . <p> Without the Oregon and Washington moorings and the network of underwater gliders the Ocean Observatories Initiative operated in the region , researchers say they 'll lose much of their ability to measure what 's happening below the surface , which is precisely where the most significant oceanographic signals are . <p> What 's next : <p> One significant piece will remain : a seafloor cable network managed by the University of Washington off the Pacific Northwest coast , which will continue providing data on volcanic and seismic activity in the region . <h> What is the Ocean Observatories Initiative ? <p> The backstory : <p> The Ocean Observatories Initiative launched in 2015 after more than 10 years of community planning and construction . Its data has been freely available and has informed more than 500 scientific publications . <p> It was designed as a 25 to 30-year project , built in part around the oceanographic consensus that detecting meaningful climate signals requires at least three decades of continuous data . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ record , " Dever said , " which will give you some hints , but it wo n't continue on . " <p> By the numbers : <p> The initiative 's annual budget was about $48 million a year , not including the cost of research vessels , which adds substantially to the overall price . Prior to budget cuts , around 60 to 70 people worked directly on the project across its partner institutions , Dever said . <p> Big picture view : <p> " What 's happening with the Ocean Observatories Initiative is not unique , " Dever said . " This is just one of a number of science facilities that is being dismantled at the present time . It seems to really mark the end of a federal commitment to basic scientific research -- a commitment that has served this nation very well for the last 70 years . @@137915084 <p> The UAE 's launch of Naseej , the National Initiative for Textile Circularity , will test whether a country known for malls , fast fashion , and high consumption can turn textile waste from an environmental afterthought into part of a working circular system . <p> The initiative , launched under the directives of President Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan , comes as the UAE is estimated to generate 220,000 metric tons of textile waste each year . It aims to create a more organized national system for collecting , reusing , recycling , and reducing textile waste while linking government agencies , businesses , researchers , recyclers , community organizations , and consumers . <p> Circular textile systems aim to keep garments and fibers in use for as long as possible through reuse , repair , resale , upcycling , recycling , and reduced waste . <p> That goal is easy to state and difficult to deliver . Across the UAE 's sustainable fashion and textile recovery ecosystem , business owners and advocates broadly welcome Naseej as an important national step . <p> But they also warn @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ country builds accessible collection systems , supports resale and repair , reduces overconsumption , and develops local capacity to handle materials that currently have few viable end-of-life options . <p> JULY 20 : A man arranges fabric at a store on July 20 , 2020 in Dubai , United Arab Emirates . The Coronavirus ( COVID-19 ) pandemic has spread to many countries across the world , claiming over 600,000 lives and infecting over 14 million people . ( credit : Francois Nel/ Getty Images ) <h> 220,000 tons of clothing go into landfills in the UAE <p> Naseej brings together the National Projects Office , the Ministry of Economy and Tourism , Emirates Foundation , Tadweer Group , researchers , businesses , and community partners to work on collection , recycling , consumer behavior , regulation , and circular business models . <p> In practical terms , the initiative is expected to support national programs , improve collection and recycling infrastructure , advance pilot projects , and help create markets for circular textile solutions . <p> Work on the initiative began during COP28 and included memorandums of understanding with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ manufacturers , recyclers , research institutions , and community organizations . Its first public activation , " The Fabric of Possibility , " is scheduled for June 5 to 7 at Yas Mall in Abu Dhabi before similar events expand to other parts of the country . <p> For Jennifer Sault , founder and managing director of Thrift for Good , the urgency is already visible in the volume of unwanted clothing moving through the UAE . <p> " An estimated 220,000 metric tons of clothing is going into landfill currently in the UAE . This is by a recent report that just came out on Naseej , the National Initiative for Textile Circularity , " Sault told The Media Line . <p> Sault said fast fashion has accelerated the problem by encouraging higher production and making clothing easier to treat as disposable . According to the UN Environment Programme , 92 million metric tons of textile waste are produced globally each year . <p> The agency has also cited Ellen MacArthur Foundation findings that clothing production doubled from 2000 to 2015 , while the duration of garment use declined @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ growing concern , not just in the UAE , but globally , as producers and consumers shift more to fast fashion , " Sault said . <p> The environmental problem , she said , is not only the quantity of discarded clothing but also what that clothing is made from . Synthetic materials such as polyester are derived from fossil fuels , shed microplastics , and can persist in the environment for decades or longer , depending on conditions . <p> The European Parliament has cited estimates that textile production is responsible for about 20% of global clean water pollution , mainly from dyeing . <p> " What 's more disturbing is that clothing is being produced much more cheaply , which means that the resources that go into it are not as good for the environment , " Sault said . <p> She voiced concern about microplastics and chemical exposure . <p> " Plastics are leaching off into waterways in our systems , into our food chains , " she said . " So it 's not just the environment , but our health as well . " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is that collection and recycling infrastructure have not kept pace with consumption . Sault said Thrift for Good has built a model that keeps nearly all of the clothing it receives in circulation through resale , repair , redistribution , stain treatment , redesign , upcycling , or recycling . But the organization 's scale is tiny compared with the national problem . <p> " We have figured out how to be 99% circular with our clothing , " she said . <p> Still , she said , the country lacks a system for many materials that are not cotton . <p> " The cottons we can do here in the UAE , Landmark Recycling Center , does a great job and has a fair amount of capacity to take this , " Sault said . " But there 's still no system in the UAE for anything that 's not cotton . So polyester blends , other materials , those that are greatly soiled , shoes , bags , accessories , etc . " <p> That limitation reflects a wider global problem . Textile recycling is technically difficult because @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be sorted and separated before their fibers can be reused . <p> A cotton shirt , a polyester dress , and a mixed-fiber garment may all require different sorting , processing , and end markets . Recycling mills also often require strict fiber quality standards , and collection systems are fragmented even in countries with advanced waste infrastructure . <p> Sault said Thrift for Good processes about 12 tons of clothing each month . About one ton goes into recycling , and roughly 400 kilograms are likely to end up in landfills . <p> " We 're quite small in terms of the scale of what 's needed in the UAE , " she said . " We 're just a scratch on the tip of an iceberg . " <p> Muhammad Virji , director of Universal Clothing and founder of Fashion Rerun and Efaar , welcomed Naseej as a step toward a more organized circular textile industry . <p> " It is an important step toward building a stronger circular textile industry and encouraging more sustainable use of clothing and textiles across the country , " Virji told The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ value that remains in clothing after its first use . He said discarded garments should not be treated automatically as waste when they can still be reused , resold , upcycled , recycled , or sorted for another purpose . <p> " Many clothes and textiles still have value after their first use , " he said . <p> The practical barriers , he said , are awareness , convenience , and collection . Many consumers may want to make better choices , but do not know where to take unwanted clothing or what happens after they dispose of it . <p> " Making collection and recycling easier can help increase participation , " he said . <p> Virji said responsibility must be shared among consumers , retailers , brands , policymakers , recyclers , and reuse businesses . Consumers can care for garments and use resale or recycling options . Retailers and brands can educate customers and support circular initiatives . Government can connect partners and help build the systems that allow those efforts to scale . <p> The UAE already has companies and community groups working in resale @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , he said . The next step is linking them into a larger chain . <p> " The opportunity now is to continue connecting these efforts so more textiles stay in use for longer , " Virji said . <p> His companies operate across different stages of that chain . Universal Clothing sorts and grades textiles so they can be directed to appropriate uses . Fashion Rerun focuses on resale . Efaar transforms existing textiles into new products through rework and upcycling . <p> Araceli Gallego , founder of GoShopia.com and Fashion Revolution UAE country coordinator , said Naseej is a positive step because it recognizes textile waste as a national issue . But she said the success of circular fashion will depend on whether the initiative moves beyond recycling and supports the community-level work that changes behavior . <p> " The launch of Naseej is a very positive step for the UAE and an important recognition of the need to address textile waste at a national level , " Gallego told The Media Line . " At Fashion Revolution UAE , we believe circularity goes far beyond recycling . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ swaps , repair and mending sessions , styling masterclasses , workshops , and community events . The goal , she said , is to extend the life of garments and keep textiles out of landfills while giving consumers practical alternatives to buying new . <p> " We also work closely with sustainable fashion designers , upcyclers , thrift shops , and stylists to promote more conscious ways of producing and consuming fashion , " she said . <h> A small but growing ecosystem contributing to textile circularity <p> Community initiatives are still small , but Gallego said they are helping create a culture around repair , reuse , and sustainable design . " However , high retail rents often mean that only large brands can secure space , leaving many sustainable labels without a presence in these prime locations . " <p> Repair , resale , rental , and upcycling are expanding , she said , but they still lack the scale and convenience of buying something new . <p> The fast-fashion question , the interviewees said , is not whether people should stop enjoying clothing , but whether the system can make better choices easier . Price , convenience , variety , climate , children outgrowing clothing , and limited access to affordable , sustainable alternatives all help explain why consumers continue to buy fast fashion even when they know the environmental costs . <p> That market reality is not unique to the UAE . Fast fashion remains dominant not simply because consumers ignore sustainability concerns , but because it offers price , access , variety , and convenience . Kristen Classi-Zummo , an apparel industry analyst at Circana , made a similar point in comments to The Washington Post about fast @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ benefits when other factors are equal , she said , but a large price gap or lack of convenience can quickly change the decision . <p> " If they 're then seeing a big price difference or it is not convenient , then they wo n't buy , " Classi-Zummo told the newspaper . <p> Gallego said consumers should be encouraged to buy fewer but better-quality items , extend garment life , support responsible brands , and make resale and repair part of ordinary shopping behavior . <p> " The solution is not necessarily to stop people from enjoying fashion , but to encourage more conscious consumption , " she said . <p> Virji framed the same idea as product life extension . <p> " The focus should be on extending the life of clothing , " he said . " Supporting collection , resale , reuse , upcycling , and recycling helps ensure garments stay in use for longer and reduces unnecessary waste . " <p> Sault said consumers have power through everyday purchasing decisions , but she also said companies and policymakers must act where market incentives fall @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is our vote for the world we want to live in , " she said . " <p> Government has a role , Sault said , because recycling often does not pay for itself and cheaper products can crowd out more ethical alternatives . <p> " Companies , of course , should be responsible .
